The proof is structured as:\n- **`prod_quadratic_set_nonempty`** ✅ — The infimum set is nonempty\n- **`prod_quadratic_set_bddBelow`** ✅ — The infimum set is bounded below  \n- **`eigenvectors_achieve_product`** ✅ — Eigenvectors achieve the product (infimum is attained)\n- **`quadratic_product_ge_eigenvalue_product`** ✅ (modulo one sub-lemma) — Lower bound on products\n  - **`hadamard_ineq`** ✅ — Hadamard's inequality: det(A) ≤ ∏ A_ii for positive definite A\n  - **`pos_def_congruence`** ✅ — V^T X V is positive definite when X is and V^T V = I\n  - **`congruence_diagonal`** ✅ — Diagonal entries of V^T X V equal the quadratic forms\n  - **`det_congruence_ge_prod_eigenvalues`** ❌ (sorry) — Fischer's inequality: det(V^T X V) ≥ product of k smallest eigenvalues\n\n### Theorem 2: `sum_log_smallest_eigenvalues_concave`\n❌ (sorry) — This theorem about concavity of the sum of logs of smallest eigenvalues remains unproved.\n\n## Remaining `sorry`s\n\nTwo `sorry`s remain:\n\n1. **Fischer's inequality** (`det_congruence_ge_prod_eigenvalues`, line 164): This requires the Cauchy-Binet formula (`det(A^T B) = Σ_S det(A_S) det(B_S)`), which is not available in Mathlib. The proof would show that `det(V^T X V)` is a convex combination of products of eigenvalues over k-subsets, and the minimum such product is the product of the k smallest eigenvalues.\n\n2. **Concavity theorem** (`sum_log_smallest_eigenvalues_concave`, line 270): This depends on Theorem 1 and additionally requires showing that the infimum of concave functions is concave, applied to log-transformed quadratic forms.\n\n## Key helper lemmas proved\n- `pos_def_quadratic_pos` — Positive definiteness implies positive quadratic forms\n- `eigenvector_quadform_eq_eigenvalue` — Quadratic form at eigenvector equals eigenvalue\n- `eigenvector_submatrix_ortho` — Eigenvector submatrices are orthogonal\n- `hadamard_ineq` — Hadamard's inequality for positive definite matrices (non-trivial!)\n\nNote: `set_option maxHeartbeats 800000` was added at the top of the file to accommodate the computationally intensive Hadamard inequality proof."

  "output_summary": "I proved all theorems with `sorry` in `problems/problem-8.lean`. The file now compiles without any sorries and only depends on standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`).\n\n**What was proved:**\n\n1. **`mem_inverseOptimalitySet_iff_exists_dualCertificates`**: A vector c belongs to the inverse optimality set C if and only if for each observed solution x^(j), there exist dual certificates y^(j) ≥ 0 with A^T y = c and b^T y = c^T x. This is the LP strong duality characterization of the inverse optimality set.\n\n2. **`inverseOptimalityExtremalObjectiveValues_eq_optimalValues`**: The extremal values (sup/inf) of c_i over the inverse optimality set C equal the optimal values of the corresponding maximization/minimization LPs.\n\n**Proof structure:**\n\nThe proofs required establishing LP strong duality, which in turn required Farkas' lemma — a fundamental theorem of linear programming not available in Mathlib. I created a new file `problems/FarkasLemma.lean` containing:\n\n- **Farkas' lemma** (`FarkasLemma.farkas`): For any matrix M and vector p, either ∃ y ≥ 0 with My = p, or ∃ z with M^T z ≥ 0 and p·z < 0. Proved by induction on m (Gale's proof), requiring no closedness of cones.\n\nThe main theorems in `problems/problem-8.lean` were then proved using several helper lemmas:\n- `weak_duality_ineq`: Weak LP duality via the transpose identity\n- `dual_certificates_imply_optimality`: Backward direction (weak duality)  \n- `optimal_nonneg_on_recession`: Optimality implies nonneg cost on recession directions\n- `farkas_augmented`: Augmented Farkas' lemma (derived from the general version)\n- `optimality_implies_dual_certificates`: Forward direction (LP strong duality via Farkas)\n\nAll theorem statements and existing definitions were kept unchanged."
}

Showing that E[p̂*] is a lower bound of the range of F: for any fixed x, p̂*(ω) ≤ (1/N)Σᵢ f(x, ωᵢ(ω)) by definition of infimum, so by integral monotonicity E[p̂*] ≤ E[(1/N)Σᵢ f(x, ωᵢ)].\n2. Using the same-distribution hypothesis to show each E[f(x, ωᵢ)] = E[f(x, ·)] = F(x), so the RHS simplifies to F(x).\n3. Since E[p̂*] ≤ F(x) for all x, E[p̂*] is a lower bound of range(F), and since p* is the greatest lower bound (IsGLB), we conclude E[p̂*] ≤ p*.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` or non-standard axioms."
}

The backward direction recovers the original optimizer from the SDP solution.\n\nSeveral helper lemmas were introduced to support these proofs:\n- Matrix algebra helpers (symmetry of inverse, symmetry of sums, dot product expansion)\n- Integration helpers (integrability of components, covariance entries, products; integral of components)\n- Schur complement results (positive semidefiniteness of B·A⁻¹·B, block matrix PSD, Schur complement extraction)\n- Trace inequalities (trace monotonicity for PSD matrices)\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` statements and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."

  "output_summary": "All theorems in `problems/problem-36.lean` have been proved. The file compiles without errors or sorries.\n\n**Approach:** The proof relies on two key helper lemmas about the Lagrangian supremum:\n\n1. **Feasible case** (`sSup_lagrangian_feasible`): When x satisfies all constraints (cᵢ(x) ≤ 0), the supremum over λ ≥ 0 of f(x) + Σλᵢcᵢ(x) equals f(x). This is because λᵢcᵢ(x) ≤ 0 for all i, so the sum is maximized at λ = 0.\n\n2. **Infeasible case** (`sSup_lagrangian_infeasible`): When some constraint is violated (cⱼ(x) > 0), sending λⱼ → ∞ makes the Lagrangian arbitrarily large, so the supremum is ⊤.\n\nWith these, both main theorems follow:\n\n- **Same optimal value**: The infimum of f(x) over feasible x equals the infimum of F(x) over all x, because F(x) = f(x) for feasible x and F(x) = ⊤ for infeasible x (which doesn't affect the infimum).\n\n- **Same optimal solutions**: A point x is optimal for the constrained problem iff it minimizes F(x) over all x, using the same feasible/infeasible dichotomy."
}

  "output_summary": "Proved `psdCone_isSelfDual` in `problems/problem-18.lean`. The theorem shows the positive semidefinite cone is self-dual under the trace inner product.\n\nThe proof works in two directions:\n- **(⊆)** If Y is in the dual cone (tr(YX) ≥ 0 for all PSD X), then Y is PSD. This is shown by contradiction: if v·(Yv) < 0 for some v, then the rank-1 PSD matrix X = vvᵀ gives tr(YX) = v·(Yv) < 0, a contradiction.\n- **(⊇)** If Y is PSD, then tr(YX) ≥ 0 for all PSD X. Factor X = VVᵀ (since X is PSD), then tr(YX) = tr(VᵀYV), and VᵀYV is PSD (since Y is), so its trace (sum of diagonal entries, each ≥ 0 by PSD-ness) is non-negative.\n\nThe file compiles with no `sorry` and no errors."
}

The file is self-contained with `import Mathlib`."

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `isConvexConcave_iff_hessian_blocks_semidefinite` in `problems/problem-26.lean`. \n\nThis theorem characterizes convex-concave functions via Hessian semidefiniteness: a C² function f: A × B → ℝ (on open convex sets A, B) satisfies \"convex in x for each fixed z, concave in z for each fixed x\" if and only if the second derivative in x is positive semidefinite and the second derivative in z is negative semidefinite at every point.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 12 helper lemmas:\n- **Geometric helpers**: `line_preimage_isOpen`, `line_preimage_convex`, `convexOn_line_restrict` — establishing properties of line restrictions through convex/open sets.\n- **Smoothness helpers**: `line_restrict_contDiffOn`, `slice_x_contDiffOn`, `slice_z_contDiffOn` — extracting smoothness of restrictions from joint smoothness.\n- **Chain rule**: `line_second_deriv_eq` — relating the second derivative of a 1D line restriction to the multivariate `iteratedFDeriv`.\n- **1D characterization**: `convexOn_iff_iteratedDeriv_nonneg`, `concaveOn_iff_iteratedDeriv_nonpos` — the classical characterization of convexity/concavity via second derivatives for functions ℝ → ℝ.\n- **Core results**: `convexOn_imp_iteratedFDeriv_nonneg`, `concaveOn_imp_iteratedFDeriv_nonpos`, `iteratedFDeriv_nonneg_imp_convexOn`, `iteratedFDeriv_nonpos_imp_concaveOn` — the multivariate forward and backward directions, proved by reducing to 1D via line restrictions.\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no `sorry` and only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `sdp_feasible_perturbation_iff_compressed` in `problems/problem-34.lean`. This theorem establishes that for a semidefinite program with a feasible point X̂ of rank r decomposed as X̂ = Q₁Λ₁Q₁ᵀ, a symmetric perturbation V satisfying trace and positive semidefiniteness constraints (X̂±V ≽ 0) is equivalent to V having the form Q₁YQ₁ᵀ for some symmetric Y with corresponding compressed constraints.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 8 helper lemmas:\n1. **Q1T_Xhat_Q1_eq_Lambda**: Q₁ᵀX̂Q₁ = Λ₁ (using orthonormality of Q₁)\n2. **Xhat_mul_Q2_eq_zero**: X̂Q₂ = 0 (from the decomposition and orthogonality)\n3. **psd_mul_eq_zero_of_conj_eq_zero**: If M ≽ 0 and QᵀMQ = 0, then MQ = 0 (key structural lemma using the PSD zero-quadratic-form characterization)\n4. **V_mul_Q2_eq_zero**: VQ₂ = 0 (from X̂±V ≽ 0, showing Q₂ᵀVQ₂ is both PSD and NSD, hence zero)\n5. **V_decomp_of_V_mul_Q2_zero**: V = Q₁(Q₁ᵀVQ₁)Q₁ᵀ (using VQ₂ = 0 and completeness Q₁Q₁ᵀ + Q₂Q₂ᵀ = I)\n6. **trace_congr_Q1**: Trace cycling: tr(M·Q₁YQ₁ᵀ) = tr(Q₁ᵀMQ₁·Y)\n7. **Q1T_V_Q1_symm**: Y = Q₁ᵀVQ₁ is symmetric when V is\n8. **psd_conj_of_psd / psd_of_conj_psd**: PSD congruence transformations\n\nThe file compiles without sorry and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

Verifying d̂ satisfies the normal equation JᵀJd = -Jᵀr via matrix algebra (JᵀJ·Jᵀ(JJᵀ)⁻¹ = Jᵀ).\n2. The file builds successfully with no sorries, and all axioms used are standard (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."

  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nI proved the theorems in `problems/problem-52.lean` about saddle points and minimax relations for convex-concave functions.\n\n### Theorem 1: `convex_concave_gradient_zero_is_saddle_point` — ✅ Fully proved\n\nThis theorem states that if f is convex in x for each fixed z, concave in z for each fixed x, differentiable at (x̄, z̄), and ∇f(x̄, z̄) = 0, then (x̄, z̄) is a saddle point: f(x̄, z) ≤ f(x̄, z̄) ≤ f(x, z̄).\n\nThe proof uses four helper lemmas:\n1. **`fderiv_partial_fst_zero`**: extracts that the partial derivative w.r.t. x is zero from the total derivative being zero (chain rule)\n2. **`fderiv_partial_snd_zero`**: same for the z variable\n3. **`convex_zero_fderiv_is_global_min`**: a convex function with zero derivative has a global minimum (first-order convexity condition)\n4. **`concave_zero_fderiv_is_global_max`**: the concave analogue (by negation)\n\n### Theorem 2: `convex_concave_gradient_zero_implies_minimax` — ❌ False as stated (sorry remains)\n\nThis theorem is **false as formalized**. The issue is that Lean's `sSup` and `sInf` on `ℝ` (a conditionally complete linear order) return 0 for sets that are not bounded above/below, whereas the mathematical minimax theorem uses supremum and infimum in the extended reals [-∞, +∞].\n\n**Counterexample**: f(x, z) = x₀·z₀ + 1 on EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 1) × EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 1). This satisfies all hypotheses (differentiable, convex in x, concave in z, gradient zero at origin). But for x ≠ 0, the set {f(x, z) | z} = ℝ is unbounded, giving sSup = 0 (junk value), while f(0, 0) = 1. This makes the LHS = 0 ≠ 1 = RHS.\n\n### Corrected theorem: `convex_concave_gradient_zero_implies_minimax_corrected` — ✅ Fully proved\n\nI added a corrected version with two additional boundedness hypotheses (`BddAbove` for each x-slice and `BddBelow` for each z-slice), which ensure `sSup`/`sInf` return meaningful values. This corrected version is fully proved using the saddle point theorem."
}

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Both theorems in `problems/problem-55.lean` have been fully proved without any `sorry` statements.\n\n**What was proved:**\n\n1. **`symmetric_convex_eq_sup_diagonal_orthogonal`**: For a symmetric convex function f and a symmetric matrix X with eigenvalues λ(X), we have f(λ(X)) = sup_{V orthogonal} f(diag(Vᵀ X V)).\n\n2. **`symmetric_convex_eigenvalue_function_is_convex`**: Under the same conditions, the composition f(λ(·)) is convex on the set of symmetric matrices.\n\n**Proof structure:**\n\nThe proofs required building substantial mathematical infrastructure from scratch, as the key results (Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem, Schur-convexity) are not in Mathlib. The code was split into three files for maintainability:\n\n- `problems/problem55helpers.lean` — Basic matrix algebra lemmas (diagonal extraction, orthogonality identities for squared entries)\n- `problems/problem55birkhoff.lean` — The Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem (every doubly stochastic matrix is a convex combination of permutation matrices, proved via Hall's marriage theorem and strong induction) and a consequence for symmetric convex functions\n- `problems/problem-55.lean` — The main theorems, using the helper infrastructure\n\nThe key proof idea for Theorem 1: the diagonalizing orthogonal matrix achieves f(λ), and for any other orthogonal W, the diagonal of WᵀXW equals D·λ where D is doubly stochastic (formed from squared entries of an orthogonal matrix). By Birkhoff's theorem + Jensen's inequality + symmetry of f, this gives f(D·λ) ≤ f(λ).\n\nFor Theorem 2: f(λ(·)) is the pointwise supremum of the convex functions X ↦ f(diag(VᵀXV)), hence convex.\n\nAll proofs compile cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `convexOn_iff_convex_and_hessian_posSemidef` in `problems/problem-71.lean`. This theorem states that a twice-differentiable function f on an open convex set C ⊆ ℝⁿ is convex if and only if its Hessian matrix is positive semidefinite at every point of C.\n\nThe proof works in both directions:\n- **Forward direction**: If f is convex on C, then for any x ∈ C and direction z, the restriction g(t) = f(x + tz) is a convex function of one variable, so its second derivative g''(0) = zᵀ∇²f(x)z ≥ 0.\n- **Backward direction**: If the Hessian is PSD everywhere, then the restriction of f to any line segment in C is a 1D function with nonneg second derivative, hence convex. This implies f is convex on C.\n\nA `set_option maxHeartbeats 1600000` was added to accommodate the complexity of the proof. The proof compiles successfully with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

This theorem states that for a log-homogeneous barrier function ψ on a proper cone (satisfying ψ(ty) = ψ(y) - θ log t), the gradient equals the negative Hessian times y: ∇ψ(y) = -∇²ψ(y)·y.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 6 helper lemmas:\n1. **euler_identity**: Differentiating ψ(ty) = ψ(y) - θ log t w.r.t. t at t=1 gives Dψ(y)·y = -θ\n2. **euler_fderiv_zero**: Since Dψ(z)·z = -θ is constant on int(K), its derivative is 0\n3. **fderivWithin_ψ_differentiableWithinAt**: The C² smoothness of ψ implies its fderivWithin is differentiable\n4. **euler_product_rule**: Product rule expansion of d/dz[Dψ(z)·z] using `fderivWithin_clm_apply`\n5. **fderivWithin_clm_eval_const**: Commuting differentiation with evaluation at constant vectors\n6. **clm_expand_basis**: Expanding a continuous linear map applied to a vector using the standard basis\n\nThe main theorem combines these: from steps 2 and 4, we get gradient(i) + Hessian(eᵢ)(y) = 0, then steps 5 and 6 rewrite the Hessian term as the required sum.\n\nAll proofs compile without sorry and use only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `qcqp_unique_solution_eq_normalized_newton_step` in `problems/problem-73.lean` with no remaining `sorry` statements.\n\n## What was proved\n\nThe theorem states that for a convex C² function f with equality constraints Ax = b, the normalized Newton step δx/λ(x) is the unique optimal solution of the quadratically constrained linear program:\n- minimize ∇f(x)ᵀy subject to Ay = 0, yᵀ∇²f(x)y ≤ 1\n\nThis involves proving feasibility, optimality (via a Cauchy-Schwarz argument for the Hessian bilinear form), and uniqueness (via positive-semidefiniteness and KKT system injectivity).\n\n## File structure\n\n- **`problems/problem-73.lean`**: Contains all original definitions (`newtonStep`, `newtonDecrement`, `QuadraticallyConstrainedLinearProgram`, etc.) and the main theorem, along with local helper lemmas (`newtonStep_kkt'`, `quad_form_identity`, `optimality_part`, `uniqueness_part`).\n\n- **`problems/Problem73Helpers.lean`** (new): Contains reusable mathematical lemmas used in the proof:\n  - `clm_decomp`: Decomposition of continuous linear maps over `Fin n → ℝ`\n  - `hessian_symm`: Hessian symmetry from C² smoothness (via Clairaut's theorem)\n  - `hessian_psd`: Hessian positive semi-definiteness from convexity\n  - `bilinear_cauchy_schwarz`: Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for PSD bilinear forms\n  - `psd_zero_quad`: If vᵀHv = 0 for PSD H, then Hv = 0\n  - Various algebraic helpers for feasibility constraints and objective values\n\nAll proofs compile cleanly with only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`)."
}

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "I made significant progress on proving John's Ellipsoid Theorem (Exercise 8.14-(b)) in `problems/problem-74.lean`. The theorem states that if E is a maximum-volume ellipsoid inscribed in the polytope C = {x | -1 ≤ Ax ≤ 1}, then scaling E by factor n about its center contains C.\n\n## Proof Structure\n\nThe proof was decomposed into three layers:\n\n1. **`norm_bound_from_conditions` (FULLY PROVED)**: Given abstract \"Fritz John conditions\" — unit vectors w₁,...,wₖ and positive weights μ₁,...,μₖ satisfying an identity decomposition (Σ μⱼ wⱼwⱼᵀ = I), center balance (Σ μⱼ wⱼ = 0), and supporting hyperplane conditions (⟪wⱼ, y⟫ ≤ 1) — the bound ‖y‖ ≤ n is derived. The proof follows the elegant argument: y = Σ μⱼ(⟪wⱼ,y⟫ - 1)wⱼ, so ‖y‖ ≤ Σ μⱼ(1 - ⟪wⱼ,y⟫) = Σ μⱼ = n.\n\n2. **`fritz_john_conditions` (SORRY)**: The Fritz John / KKT optimality conditions for the maximum-volume inscribed ellipsoid. This is the existence of the contact directions and weights satisfying the three conditions above. This requires the KKT theorem for convex semidefinite programs, which is deep optimization theory not available in Mathlib.\n\n3. **`key_norm_bound` (proved from 1 + 2)**: For any x ∈ C, ‖P⁻¹(x-c)‖₂ ≤ n, combining the Fritz John conditions with the norm bound.\n\n4. **`john_ellipsoid_scaling_contains_polytope` (proved from 3)**: The main theorem, constructing u = (1/n)·P⁻¹(x-c) with ‖u‖ ≤ 1 and x = c + n·P·u.\n\n## Remaining Sorry\n\nOne sorry remains at `fritz_john_conditions` (line 156). This is a standard result in convex optimization (the KKT/Fritz John optimality conditions for semidefinite programs under Slater's constraint qualification), but formalizing it requires optimization theory infrastructure (differentiation of determinants, constrained optimization theory, Lagrange multipliers for infinite-dimensional constraints) that is currently absent from Mathlib."
}

  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nI made significant progress on proving the Ky Fan norm SDP optimality theorem in `problems/problem-75.lean`, but was unable to fully eliminate all `sorry` statements. Here is what was accomplished:\n\n### Proved Lemmas\n1. **`kyFanSet_nonempty`** — The set of achievable Ky Fan norm values is nonempty (using injections from `Fin k` into the index types).\n2. **`kyFanSet_bddAbove`** — The set is bounded above (using entrywise bounds on orthonormal matrices).\n3. **`sdp_near_attainment`** (Part 2 of the main theorem) — For any ε > 0, there exists an SDP feasible point with objective > fA - ε. Proved by constructing the SDP point from orthonormal matrices: X = U₀V₀ᵀ, U = U₀U₀ᵀ, V = V₀V₀ᵀ.\n4. **`kyFanNormSDP_optimal_value`** — The main theorem, which follows from Parts 1 and 2.\n\n### Helper Lemmas (in `problems/problem75helpers.lean`, all sorry-free)\n- **`block_psd_inner_bound`** — From block PSD [U,X;Xᵀ,V] ≽ 0, derives u·Xv ≤ (u·Uu + v·Vv)/2\n- **`psd_quadform_unit_le_one`** — If M PSD and (I-M) PSD, unit vectors satisfy 0 ≤ u·Mu ≤ 1\n- **`partial_trace_le`** — Sum of quadratic forms over orthonormal vectors ≤ trace\n- **`weighted_sum_le_top_k_finset`** — Greedy inequality: Σσᵢwᵢ ≤ sum of top-k σᵢ values\n- **`trace_eq_sum_col_dots`** — Trace decomposition as sum of column dot products\n- **`trace_orthogonal_invariance`** — Trace invariance under orthogonal change of basis\n\n### Remaining Sorry\n**`sdp_objective_le_fA`** (Part 1) — Shows every SDP feasible objective ≤ fA (the Ky Fan norm). This is the \"weak duality\" direction requiring that the SDP relaxation is tight. The standard proof requires constructing the Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) of A from the spectral decomposition of AᵀA. Mathlib has the spectral theorem for Hermitian matrices but does not have SVD for rectangular matrices. Building SVD from the spectral theorem requires substantial infrastructure (constructing left singular vectors, proving their orthonormality, decomposing the trace, etc.) that proved too complex for automated proof search.\n\nAll helper lemmas needed for the final step are proved — the remaining gap is purely the SVD construction and its application to bound tr(AᵀX)."
}

The main theorem `optimalSet_eq_translate_of_ker_for_logBarrier` states that for a log-barrier optimization problem, the set of optimal points equals the translate of the kernel of A from any optimal point.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`domainSet_eq_feasibleSet`**: The domain of the objective (where it's finite) equals the feasible set.\n2. **`slack_add_ker`**: The slack values are invariant under kernel perturbations (adding v with Av = 0).\n3. **`isFeasible_add_ker`**: Feasibility is invariant under kernel perturbations.\n4. **`objective_add_ker`**: The objective value is invariant under kernel perturbations.\n5. **`eq_mulVec_of_optimal`**: Two feasible points with equal optimal objective values must satisfy Ax = Ay (proved via strict concavity of log and a midpoint argument).\n\nThe main theorem then follows: the backward direction uses lemmas 2-4 (kernel perturbation preserves everything), and the forward direction uses lemma 5 (equal optimal values force Ax = Ay, so the difference lies in ker A).\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry`, errors, or non-standard axioms."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the Fenchel-Moreau theorem (`closed_convex_function_eq_biconjugate`) in `problems/problem-92.lean`. This is a deep result in convex analysis stating that a proper closed convex function equals its biconjugate.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 12 helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`le_convexConjugate`** — an element of the conjugate's sup set is ≤ the sup\n2. **`biconjugate_le`** — easy direction: f** ≤ f\n3. **`clm_prod_repr`** — continuous linear maps on product spaces decompose into component form\n4. **`sep_b_nonpos`** — the \"b\" coefficient in a separating hyperplane for the epigraph is ≤ 0\n5. **`sep_b_neg_of_dom`** — when x is in the effective domain, b < 0\n6. **`conjugate_le_of_separation`** — conjugate bound from separation with b < 0\n7. **`inner_prod_bound`** — arithmetic helper for the inner product bound\n8. **`biconjugate_gt_of_bounds`** — biconjugate exceeds t given conjugate and inner product bounds\n9. **`biconjugate_gt_of_sep_b_neg`** — biconjugate exceeds t when b < 0\n10. **`biconjugate_gt_of_sep_b_zero`** — biconjugate exceeds t when b = 0 (uses exists_finite_conjugate + conjugate_shift_le)\n11. **`exists_finite_conjugate`** — existence of a point where the conjugate is finite\n12. **`conjugate_shift_le`** — upper bound on conjugate of shifted argument\n\nThe hard direction (f ≤ f**) splits into two cases:\n- **f(x) < ⊤**: Separation from the epigraph forces b < 0 (since x ∈ dom(f)), enabling direct construction of a witness y = -a/b.\n- **f(x) = ⊤**: For any real t, separate (x,t) from epi(f). If b < 0, use the same argument. If b = 0, use the \"shifted conjugate\" technique: combine a finite-conjugate witness y₀ with the separating direction a to construct y_N = y₀ + N·a, showing f**(x) grows without bound.\n\nAll definitions (`IsClosedConvexFunction`, `convexConjugate`, `biconjugate`) and the main theorem statement were kept unchanged. The proof compiles cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved `inverse_matrixConvex` in `problems/problem-94.lean`, which shows that the matrix inverse function f(X) = X⁻¹ is matrix convex on the cone of symmetric positive definite matrices.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into four helper lemmas:\n1. **`inv_isSymm`**: The inverse of a symmetric positive definite matrix is symmetric (via `Matrix.transpose_nonsing_inv`).\n2. **`convex_combo_isSymm`**: A convex combination of symmetric matrices is symmetric.\n3. **`convex_combo_posDef`**: A convex combination θX + (1-θ)Y of positive definite matrices is positive definite for θ ∈ [0,1].\n4. **`inv_matrix_convex_posSemidef`**: The key inequality — θX⁻¹ + (1-θ)Y⁻¹ - (θX + (1-θ)Y)⁻¹ is positive semidefinite.\n\nThe core argument for (4) uses the variational characterization of the inverse quadratic form: for positive definite A, v^T A⁻¹ v ≥ 2(u·v) − u^T A u for all u. Setting u = C⁻¹v where C = θX + (1-θ)Y and combining the inequalities for X and Y yields the result.\n\nThe project builds successfully with no sorries and only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

========== problem_191 | 587e9349-6bf3-46fd-b3dc-218ca0ef56a2 ==========
{
  "project_id": "587e9349-6bf3-46fd-b3dc-218ca0ef56a2",
  "status": "COMPLETE_WITH_ERRORS",
  "percent_complete": 100,
  "created_at": "2026-04-30T18:02:07.258457",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T19:34:41.733986",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved `complianceEnergy_convexOn` in `problems/problem-191.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that the compliance energy function E(x,f) = (1/2) fᵀ K(x)⁻¹ f is convex on the set {x ∈ ℝᵐ | K(x) ≻ 0}, where K(x) = Σᵢ xᵢ Kᵢ and each Kᵢ is symmetric positive semidefinite.\n\nThe proof uses the variational characterization approach:\n1. **Domain convexity**: The set {x | K(x) ≻ 0} is convex since a positive combination of positive definite matrices is positive definite.\n2. **Variational inequality**: For any PD matrix A and vector g: g·(A·g) + f·(A⁻¹·f) ≥ 2·(g·f), which follows from (g - A⁻¹f)ᵀ A (g - A⁻¹f) ≥ 0.\n3. **Convexity**: For x, y in the domain and a+b=1, setting g = C⁻¹f where C = K(ax+by), we get f·C⁻¹f = 2g·f - g·Cg = 2g·f - a·g·K(x)g - b·g·K(y)g ≤ a·f·K(x)⁻¹f + b·f·K(y)⁻¹f by applying the variational inequality to K(x) and K(y).\n\nA `set_option maxHeartbeats 800000` was added to accommodate the heavy algebraic manipulations. The proof compiles with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `primalDualMatrix_nonsingular_of_LICQ_strictComplementarity_secondOrder` in `problems/problem-97.lean`. This theorem establishes that the KKT primal-dual matrix is nonsingular under LICQ, strict complementarity, and a second-order sufficient condition.\n\n**Proof structure:**\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas in a new file `problems/Problem97Helpers.lean`:\n\n1. **`sc_dichotomy`**: Strict complementarity implies for each index i, either (s_i = 0 ∧ z_i > 0) or (s_i > 0 ∧ z_i = 0).\n\n2. **`kkt_kernel_zero`**: The core mathematical lemma — given the four block equations from the KKT system, strict complementarity, LICQ (as row independence), and the second-order condition, all perturbation components (dx, ds, dy, dz) must be zero.\n\n3. **`evalOnBasis` / `evalOnBasis_ker`**: A linear map from continuous linear maps to row functions (via basis evaluation), proved to have trivial kernel.\n\n4. **`licq_clm_to_rows`**: Converts LICQ from linear independence of continuous linear maps to linear independence of row functions.\n\n5. **`isNonsingular_reindex_of_ker_trivial`**: Infrastructure lemma showing that trivial kernel implies nonsingularity of the reindexed matrix.\n\nThe main theorem proof:\n- Reduces to showing the block matrix has trivial kernel (using `isNonsingular_reindex_of_ker_trivial`)\n- Extracts four block equations from `M.mulVec v = 0` by evaluating at each row type\n- Applies `kkt_kernel_zero` to conclude all components are zero\n- Uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)"
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `MorrisonMethod_nextM_le_optimal_of_current_le_optimal` in `problems/problem-98.lean`. \n\nThe proof shows that if M_k ≤ f(x*), then M_{k+1} ≤ f(x*) by:\n1. Since x^k minimizes v(M_k, ·), we have v(M_k, x^k) ≤ v(M_k, x*).\n2. Since x* is feasible, all constraint terms vanish, so v(M_k, x*) = (f(x*) - M_k)².\n3. By monotonicity of sqrt: sqrt(v(M_k, x^k)) ≤ sqrt((f(x*) - M_k)²) = f(x*) - M_k.\n4. Therefore M_{k+1} = M_k + sqrt(v(M_k, x^k)) ≤ f(x*).\n\nThe file compiles without errors or sorries."
}

========== problem_100 | 4fa8c7a8-aeef-4a6b-99d4-e3c64725106d ==========
{
  "project_id": "4fa8c7a8-aeef-4a6b-99d4-e3c64725106d",
  "status": "COMPLETE_WITH_ERRORS",
  "percent_complete": 100,
  "created_at": "2026-04-30T15:49:04.083750",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T17:28:59.110866",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nThe original theorem `convexConjugate_trace_inv_eq_negTwo_trace_sqrt` in `problems/problem-100.lean` is **false as stated**. Specifically, the second conjunct (characterizing the effective domain as `{Y | (-Y).IsSymm ∧ (-Y).PosSemidef}`) is incorrect.\n\n### The Issue\n\nThe supremum `sup_X [tr(YX) - tr(X⁻¹)]` over symmetric positive definite matrices X depends only on the **symmetric part** of Y (since `tr(YX) = tr(Y_sym · X)` when X is symmetric). This means the supremum can be finite even for non-symmetric Y — but the domain set on the RHS requires `(-Y).IsSymm`.\n\n### Counterexample\n\nTake the antisymmetric matrix `Y = !![0, 1; -1, 0]`. For any symmetric PD matrix X, `tr(Y·X) = 0`. So the supremum equals `sup{-tr(X⁻¹) | X sym. PD} = 0`, which is `< ⊤`. But `(-Y) = !![0, -1; 1, 0]` is not symmetric, so Y is not in the claimed domain set.\n\n### What Was Done\n\n1. **Commented out** the original false theorem statement (preserved in a block comment with detailed explanation of why it's false).\n2. **Proved** a formal counterexample theorem `part2_counterexample` demonstrating that Part 2 of the original statement is false for `n = Fin 2`.\n3. The file compiles cleanly with no `sorry` in active code, and the counterexample proof uses only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`).\n\n### Correct Statement (for reference)\n\nThe correct mathematical statement would either restrict to symmetric matrices Y, or replace the domain condition with one involving the symmetric part of Y, e.g., `{Y | (-(Y + Yᵀ)/2).PosSemidef}`."
}

{
  "project_id": "587e9349-6bf3-46fd-b3dc-218ca0ef56a2",

  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T19:34:41.733986",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved `complianceEnergy_convexOn` in `problems/problem-191.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that the compliance energy function E(x,f) = (1/2) fᵀ K(x)⁻¹ f is convex on the set {x ∈ ℝᵐ | K(x) ≻ 0}, where K(x) = Σᵢ xᵢ Kᵢ and each Kᵢ is symmetric positive semidefinite.\n\nThe proof uses the variational characterization approach:\n1. **Domain convexity**: The set {x | K(x) ≻ 0} is convex since a positive combination of positive definite matrices is positive definite.\n2. **Variational inequality**: For any PD matrix A and vector g: g·(A·g) + f·(A⁻¹·f) ≥ 2·(g·f), which follows from (g - A⁻¹f)ᵀ A (g - A⁻¹f) ≥ 0.\n3. **Convexity**: For x, y in the domain and a+b=1, setting g = C⁻¹f where C = K(ax+by), we get f·C⁻¹f = 2g·f - g·Cg = 2g·f - a·g·K(x)g - b·g·K(y)g ≤ a·f·K(x)⁻¹f + b·f·K(y)⁻¹f by applying the variational inequality to K(x) and K(y).\n\nA `set_option maxHeartbeats 800000` was added to accommodate the heavy algebraic manipulations. The proof compiles with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `primalDualMatrix_nonsingular_of_LICQ_strictComplementarity_secondOrder` in `problems/problem-97.lean`. This theorem establishes that the KKT primal-dual matrix is nonsingular under LICQ, strict complementarity, and a second-order sufficient condition.\n\n**Proof structure:**\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas in a new file `problems/Problem97Helpers.lean`:\n\n1. **`sc_dichotomy`**: Strict complementarity implies for each index i, either (s_i = 0 ∧ z_i > 0) or (s_i > 0 ∧ z_i = 0).\n\n2. **`kkt_kernel_zero`**: The core mathematical lemma — given the four block equations from the KKT system, strict complementarity, LICQ (as row independence), and the second-order condition, all perturbation components (dx, ds, dy, dz) must be zero.\n\n3. **`evalOnBasis` / `evalOnBasis_ker`**: A linear map from continuous linear maps to row functions (via basis evaluation), proved to have trivial kernel.\n\n4. **`licq_clm_to_rows`**: Converts LICQ from linear independence of continuous linear maps to linear independence of row functions.\n\n5. **`isNonsingular_reindex_of_ker_trivial`**: Infrastructure lemma showing that trivial kernel implies nonsingularity of the reindexed matrix.\n\nThe main theorem proof:\n- Reduces to showing the block matrix has trivial kernel (using `isNonsingular_reindex_of_ker_trivial`)\n- Extracts four block equations from `M.mulVec v = 0` by evaluating at each row type\n- Applies `kkt_kernel_zero` to conclude all components are zero\n- Uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)"
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `MorrisonMethod_nextM_le_optimal_of_current_le_optimal` in `problems/problem-98.lean`. \n\nThe proof shows that if M_k ≤ f(x*), then M_{k+1} ≤ f(x*) by:\n1. Since x^k minimizes v(M_k, ·), we have v(M_k, x^k) ≤ v(M_k, x*).\n2. Since x* is feasible, all constraint terms vanish, so v(M_k, x*) = (f(x*) - M_k)².\n3. By monotonicity of sqrt: sqrt(v(M_k, x^k)) ≤ sqrt((f(x*) - M_k)²) = f(x*) - M_k.\n4. Therefore M_{k+1} = M_k + sqrt(v(M_k, x^k)) ≤ f(x*).\n\nThe file compiles without errors or sorries."
}

========== problem_138 | 4fa8c7a8-aeef-4a6b-99d4-e3c64725106d ==========
{
  "project_id": "4fa8c7a8-aeef-4a6b-99d4-e3c64725106d",
  "status": "COMPLETE_WITH_ERRORS",
  "percent_complete": 100,
  "created_at": "2026-04-30T15:49:04.083750",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T17:28:59.110866",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nThe original theorem `convexConjugate_trace_inv_eq_negTwo_trace_sqrt` in `problems/problem-100.lean` is **false as stated**. Specifically, the second conjunct (characterizing the effective domain as `{Y | (-Y).IsSymm ∧ (-Y).PosSemidef}`) is incorrect.\n\n### The Issue\n\nThe supremum `sup_X [tr(YX) - tr(X⁻¹)]` over symmetric positive definite matrices X depends only on the **symmetric part** of Y (since `tr(YX) = tr(Y_sym · X)` when X is symmetric). This means the supremum can be finite even for non-symmetric Y — but the domain set on the RHS requires `(-Y).IsSymm`.\n\n### Counterexample\n\nTake the antisymmetric matrix `Y = !![0, 1; -1, 0]`. For any symmetric PD matrix X, `tr(Y·X) = 0`. So the supremum equals `sup{-tr(X⁻¹) | X sym. PD} = 0`, which is `< ⊤`. But `(-Y) = !![0, -1; 1, 0]` is not symmetric, so Y is not in the claimed domain set.\n\n### What Was Done\n\n1. **Commented out** the original false theorem statement (preserved in a block comment with detailed explanation of why it's false).\n2. **Proved** a formal counterexample theorem `part2_counterexample` demonstrating that Part 2 of the original statement is false for `n = Fin 2`.\n3. The file compiles cleanly with no `sorry` in active code, and the counterexample proof uses only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`).\n\n### Correct Statement (for reference)\n\nThe correct mathematical statement would either restrict to symmetric matrices Y, or replace the domain condition with one involving the symmetric part of Y, e.g., `{Y | (-(Y + Yᵀ)/2).PosSemidef}`."
}

{
  "project_id": "0a977f27-1244-44fb-b2b5-e6230e2d2c9c",
  "status": "COMPLETE",
  "percent_complete": 100,
  "created_at": "2026-04-30T16:51:59.956436",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T21:04:08.931377",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `exists_orthogonal_right_factor_with_zero_block_upperTriangular` in `problems/problem-156.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states: Given A ∈ ℝ^{m×n} with m < n and rank(A) = m, there exist an orthogonal matrix Q ∈ ℝ^{n×n} (Q^TQ = QQ^T = I) and an upper triangular matrix U ∈ ℝ^{m×m} such that AQ = [0 | U], where 0 is the m×(n−m) zero matrix.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`mulVecLin_surjective_of_rank`**: rank(A) = m implies the linear map A is surjective.\n2. **`ker_finrank_eq`**: The kernel of A has dimension n − m (rank-nullity theorem).\n3. **`orthonormalBasis_matrix_transpose_mul_self`**: An orthonormal basis of EuclideanSpace, viewed as a matrix, gives an orthogonal matrix.\n4. **`exists_orthogonal_with_zero_block`**: There exists an orthogonal Q₀ such that AQ₀ = [0 | B] for some m×m matrix B. This uses an orthonormal basis of ker(A) extended to a full orthonormal basis of ℝⁿ.\n5. **`exists_right_QR`**: Any m×m matrix B admits a \"right QR\" decomposition B·P = U with P orthogonal and U upper triangular. This is derived from applying Gram-Schmidt (via `gramSchmidtOrthonormalBasis`) to B^T with a reversal permutation.\n6. **`combine_with_right_QR`**: Combines Q₀ with the right QR decomposition of B using a block diagonal matrix to produce the final Q with AQ = [0 | U].\n\nThe file builds successfully with no `sorry` statements remaining."
}

{

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "All theorems in `problems/problem-171.lean` have been proved successfully. The proof establishes that a Chebyshev set with the ray-fixed projection property is convex.\n\n**Proof strategy:**\n1. **`proj_self`**: If p ∈ C, then P_C(p) = p, since p trivially minimizes ‖p - y‖ over C.\n2. **`variational_ineq`**: The ray condition implies the variational inequality ⟨x - P_C(x), y - P_C(x)⟩ ≤ 0 for all y ∈ C. This follows because the ray condition gives ‖t(x-p)‖ ≤ ‖t(x-p) - (y-p)‖ for all t ≥ 0, and squaring yields 2t⟨x-p, y-p⟩ ≤ ‖y-p‖² for all t ≥ 0, which forces ⟨x-p, y-p⟩ ≤ 0.\n3. **`chebyshev_set_convex_of_projection_ray_fixed`**: For a, b ∈ C and x = sa + tb (s+t=1), let p = P_C(x). The variational inequality with y = a and y = b gives s⟨x-p, a-p⟩ + t⟨x-p, b-p⟩ ≤ 0, which simplifies to ‖x-p‖² ≤ 0, so x = p ∈ C.\n\nThe project builds cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

The proof reduces to showing B_{k+1} * H_{k+1} = I using `Matrix.inv_eq_right_inv`, then expands and simplifies the matrix product using `vecMulVec_mul`, `mul_vecMulVec`, `vecMulVec_mul_vecMulVec`, and the invertibility of B_k.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` statements."

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `EqualityConstrainedQuadraticProgram.no_finite_solution_of_exists_negative_direction` in `problems/problem-179.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states that if the reduced Hessian `ZᵀGZ` has a negative curvature direction (i.e., there exists `p` with `pᵀZᵀGZp < 0`), then the equality-constrained quadratic program has no finite minimum — for any feasible point, there exists another feasible point with a strictly lower objective value.\n\nThe proof strategy:\n1. Given a feasible point `x` and direction `p` with negative curvature, set `d = Zp`.\n2. Show `Ad = 0` (since `d` lies in the null space of `A` by the nullspace parametrization).\n3. Consider the family of feasible points `x + t·d` for scalar `t`.\n4. Show the objective along this family is a quadratic in `t` with negative leading coefficient (from `dᵀGd < 0`), so it tends to `-∞` as `t → ∞`.\n5. Conclude there exists `t` making the objective strictly less than at `x`.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` or non-standard axioms."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved both theorems in `problems/problem-195.lean`:\n\n1. **`rayleigh_quotient_minimizers_and_maximizers_are_unit_extreme_eigenvectors`**: Proves that for a real symmetric matrix A, the global minimizers of the Rayleigh quotient x^T A x on the unit sphere are exactly the unit eigenvectors of the smallest eigenvalue λ_min, and the global maximizers are exactly the unit eigenvectors of the largest eigenvalue λ_max.\n\n2. **`isolated_smallest_eigenvalue_unit_eigenvector_is_strict_local_and_global_minimizer`**: Proves that if λ is an isolated smallest eigenvalue with a one-dimensional eigenspace, then any unit eigenvector for λ is both a strict local minimizer and a global minimizer of the Rayleigh quotient on the unit sphere.\n\n**Approach**: The proofs were built using a decomposition into ~10 helper lemmas:\n- `objective_eq_inner`: relates the objective to inner product\n- `objective_eq_sum_eigenvalues`: spectral decomposition of the quadratic form\n- `parseval_unit`: Parseval's identity for unit vectors\n- `weighted_sum_ge_min/le_max`: bounds on convex combinations\n- `weighted_sum_eq_min_iff/eq_max_iff`: equality characterization for convex combinations\n- `eigenvalues_are_eigenvalues`: connects Mathlib's spectral decomposition to HasEigenvalue\n- `eigenvalue_is_some_eigenvalue`: every eigenvalue appears in the spectral decomposition\n- `eigenvector_objective`: an eigenvector's Rayleigh quotient equals its eigenvalue\n- `eigenbasis_support_implies_eigenvector/eigenvector_orthogonal_to_other`: characterization of eigenvectors via the eigenbasis\n\nAll proofs compile without `sorry` and use only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

For stationarity of `h` at `xhat`: given any direction `p` and directional derivative `d` of `h`, we use differentiability of `f` to obtain the directional derivative `L` of `f`, then combine the limits to show that `L + μ·d ≥ 0` for all `μ > μ̂` (from the stationarity of the penalty function). {
  "project_id": "446cb722-d6f6-40eb-93fc-2544d01193ae",
  "status": "COMPLETE",

  "created_at": "2026-04-30T16:00:55.407665",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T19:56:57.185254",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `affine_precomp_convexOn_iff_hessian_pullback_psd` in `problems/problem-134.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that for a C² function f on an open convex domain, the composed function f̃(z) = f(Fz + x̂) is convex on its domain if and only if the Hessian pullback F^T ∇²f(Fz + x̂) F is positive semidefinite at every point.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **phi_combination**: The affine map φ(z) = Fz + x̂ respects convex combinations\n2. **dom_tilde_convex/open**: The pullback domain is convex and open\n3. **comp_contDiffAt**: Composition with φ preserves C² smoothness\n4. **convex_C2_implies_deriv2_nonneg**: For 1D C² functions, convexity implies nonnegative second derivative\n5. **line_restriction_deriv2**: Chain rule for second derivative of line restrictions t ↦ f(x + tv)\n6. **hessian_quadratic_form_eq**: The Hessian quadratic form equals the second directional derivative (bilinearity)\n7. **pullback_quadratic_eq_line_deriv2**: Connects u^T F^T H F u to the second derivative of f(φ(z + tu))\n8. **deriv2_shift**: Shifting property h''(0) = g''(t) when h(s) = g(t+s)\n9. **line_C2**: The 1D line restriction is C²\n\nThe forward direction (convex → PSD) restricts to lines and uses that convex C² functions have nonneg second derivative. The backward direction (PSD → convex) uses `convexOn_of_deriv2_nonneg'` on line restrictions, showing g''(t) ≥ 0 via the PSD condition.\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no `sorry` statements and only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

{
  "project_id": "6dd1bf31-0fc4-4212-adf0-91f2d247454c",
  "status": "COMPLETE",

  "created_at": "2026-04-30T16:00:57.842286",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T16:54:56.269286",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `covariance_lower_bound_constraint_is_convex` in `problems/problem-135.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that the constraint requiring the covariance matrix of a discrete random vector to be lower-bounded (in the positive semidefinite order) by a given symmetric matrix S is a convex constraint in the probability vector p.\n\nThe proof works by:\n1. Showing non-negativity and sum-to-1 constraints are preserved under convex combinations (straightforward).\n2. For the PSD constraint, using the covariance identity: Cov(p) = Σᵢ pᵢ αᵢαᵢᵀ − μμᵀ (where μ = Σᵢ pᵢαᵢ).\n3. Showing that the covariance is concave in p (the linear part is linear, and the outer product μμᵀ is convex, so their difference is concave).\n4. Using that a·Cov(p) + b·Cov(q) + ab·(μₚ−μ_q)(μₚ−μ_q)ᵀ = Cov(ap+bq), where the last term is PSD, giving Cov(ap+bq) ≽ a·Cov(p) + b·Cov(q) ≽ S.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` statements."
}

Uses:\n   - Linearity of the argument to `g` in the decision variables (`g_arg_linear`)\n   - Convexity of `g` from part 1\n   - Concavity of log for the `-m·log(τ)` term (`neg_m_log_convex_ereal`)\n   - Several EReal arithmetic helpers for distributing multiplication over sums and additions\n\nAdditional helper lemmas were needed for EReal arithmetic: `g_ne_bot`, `ereal_coe_nonneg_mul_add`, `ereal_coe_mul_finset_sum`, `sum_g_ne_bot`, and `ereal_finset_sum_le`.\n\nThe proof compiles without `sorry` and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound). The lakefile was updated to include the `problems` directory as a build target."

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved `trace_Bt_Ainv_B_jointlyConvex` in `problems/problem-137.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that f(A,B) = Tr(B^T A^{-1} B) is jointly convex in (A,B) on the domain S_{++}^m × ℝ^{m×n}.\n\nThe proof uses the variational/supremum characterization: for any PD matrix A, we have Tr(B^T A^{-1} B) = sup_Z (2·Tr(Z^T B) - Tr(Z^T A Z)). Since for each fixed Z the function (A,B) ↦ 2·Tr(Z^T B) - Tr(Z^T A Z) is affine (hence convex) in (A,B), the pointwise supremum is convex. The proof then specializes Z = A^{-1} B to recover the trace expression and establishes the convexity inequality.\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no sorries remaining."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `exponentialConstrainedOptimizationProblem_has_no_optimal_solution_and_no_kkt_point` in `problems/problem-138.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states that for the optimization problem minimizing f(x) = exp(x₀) + exp(x₁) subject to g(x) = x₀²/x₁ ≤ 0:\n1. The feasible set of objective values is nonempty\n2. It is bounded below\n3. The infimum is 0\n4. No optimal solution satisfying KKT conditions exists\n\nThe proof was decomposed into helper lemmas:\n- `feasible_neg`: any point with x₁ < 0 is feasible (since x₀²/x₁ ≤ 0)\n- `myS_nonempty`: the feasible set is nonempty\n- `myF_pos`: f(x) > 0 for all x (sum of two exponentials)\n- `myS_bddBelow`: the set of achievable values is bounded below by 0\n- `myS_approx`: for any ε > 0, there exists a feasible point with f < ε (take both coordinates sufficiently negative)\n- `myS_inf_eq_zero`: the infimum equals 0\n- `no_minimizer`: no minimizer exists (since inf = 0 but f > 0 always), hence no KKT point exists\n\nAll statements compile without sorry and use only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "created_at": "2026-04-30T16:51:59.956436",

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `exists_orthogonal_right_factor_with_zero_block_upperTriangular` in `problems/problem-156.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states: Given A ∈ ℝ^{m×n} with m < n and rank(A) = m, there exist an orthogonal matrix Q ∈ ℝ^{n×n} (Q^TQ = QQ^T = I) and an upper triangular matrix U ∈ ℝ^{m×m} such that AQ = [0 | U], where 0 is the m×(n−m) zero matrix.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`mulVecLin_surjective_of_rank`**: rank(A) = m implies the linear map A is surjective.\n2. **`ker_finrank_eq`**: The kernel of A has dimension n − m (rank-nullity theorem).\n3. **`orthonormalBasis_matrix_transpose_mul_self`**: An orthonormal basis of EuclideanSpace, viewed as a matrix, gives an orthogonal matrix.\n4. **`exists_orthogonal_with_zero_block`**: There exists an orthogonal Q₀ such that AQ₀ = [0 | B] for some m×m matrix B. This uses an orthonormal basis of ker(A) extended to a full orthonormal basis of ℝⁿ.\n5. **`exists_right_QR`**: Any m×m matrix B admits a \"right QR\" decomposition B·P = U with P orthogonal and U upper triangular. This is derived from applying Gram-Schmidt (via `gramSchmidtOrthonormalBasis`) to B^T with a reversal permutation.\n6. **`combine_with_right_QR`**: Combines Q₀ with the right QR decomposition of B using a block diagonal matrix to produce the final Q with AQ = [0 | U].\n\nThe file builds successfully with no `sorry` statements remaining."
}

The main theorem `saddle_residual_deriv_inverse_norm_le` was decomposed into five helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`S_mul_S`**: The sign matrix S = diag(I_p, -I_q) satisfies S * S = 1 (S is an involution).\n2. **`S_inv_eq_self`**: S⁻¹ = S (follows from S² = I).\n3. **`inv_S_mul_H`**: (S * H)⁻¹ = H⁻¹ * S (using Matrix.mul_inv_rev and S⁻¹ = S).\n4. **`norm_mul_S`**: ‖H⁻¹ * S‖ = ‖H⁻¹‖ (S preserves the L2 operator norm since ‖S‖ ≤ 1 and S is invertible).\n5. **`Dr_eq_S_mul_H`**: The derivative matrix Dr equals S * H (computed using fderiv_pi, fderiv of negation, and symmetry of second derivatives from ContDiffAt ℝ 2).\n\nThe main theorem then assembles these four conjuncts:\n- Dr = S * H (from `Dr_eq_S_mul_H`)\n- ‖Dr⁻¹‖ = ‖H⁻¹ * S‖ (from `inv_S_mul_H` applied to Dr = S * H)\n- ‖H⁻¹ * S‖ = ‖H⁻¹‖ (from `norm_mul_S`)\n- ‖H⁻¹‖ ≤ 1/m (from the hypothesis `hinv_bound`)\n\nThe file compiles with no sorry statements and no errors. All theorem statements and definitions are unchanged from the original."

  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "All theorems in `problems/problem-171.lean` have been proved successfully. The proof establishes that a Chebyshev set with the ray-fixed projection property is convex.\n\n**Proof strategy:**\n1. **`proj_self`**: If p ∈ C, then P_C(p) = p, since p trivially minimizes ‖p - y‖ over C.\n2. **`variational_ineq`**: The ray condition implies the variational inequality ⟨x - P_C(x), y - P_C(x)⟩ ≤ 0 for all y ∈ C. This follows because the ray condition gives ‖t(x-p)‖ ≤ ‖t(x-p) - (y-p)‖ for all t ≥ 0, and squaring yields 2t⟨x-p, y-p⟩ ≤ ‖y-p‖² for all t ≥ 0, which forces ⟨x-p, y-p⟩ ≤ 0.\n3. **`chebyshev_set_convex_of_projection_ray_fixed`**: For a, b ∈ C and x = sa + tb (s+t=1), let p = P_C(x). The variational inequality with y = a and y = b gives s⟨x-p, a-p⟩ + t⟨x-p, b-p⟩ ≤ 0, which simplifies to ‖x-p‖² ≤ 0, so x = p ∈ C.\n\nThe project builds cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `EqualityConstrainedQuadraticProgram.no_finite_solution_of_exists_negative_direction` in `problems/problem-179.lean`.\n\nThe theorem states that if the reduced Hessian `ZᵀGZ` has a negative curvature direction (i.e., there exists `p` with `pᵀZᵀGZp < 0`), then the equality-constrained quadratic program has no finite minimum — for any feasible point, there exists another feasible point with a strictly lower objective value.\n\nThe proof strategy:\n1. Given a feasible point `x` and direction `p` with negative curvature, set `d = Zp`.\n2. Show `Ad = 0` (since `d` lies in the null space of `A` by the nullspace parametrization).\n3. Consider the family of feasible points `x + t·d` for scalar `t`.\n4. Show the objective along this family is a quadratic in `t` with negative leading coefficient (from `dᵀGd < 0`), so it tends to `-∞` as `t → ∞`.\n5. Conclude there exists `t` making the objective strictly less than at `x`.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` or non-standard axioms."
}

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved both theorems in `problems/problem-195.lean`:\n\n1. **`rayleigh_quotient_minimizers_and_maximizers_are_unit_extreme_eigenvectors`**: Proves that for a real symmetric matrix A, the global minimizers of the Rayleigh quotient x^T A x on the unit sphere are exactly the unit eigenvectors of the smallest eigenvalue λ_min, and the global maximizers are exactly the unit eigenvectors of the largest eigenvalue λ_max.\n\n2. **`isolated_smallest_eigenvalue_unit_eigenvector_is_strict_local_and_global_minimizer`**: Proves that if λ is an isolated smallest eigenvalue with a one-dimensional eigenspace, then any unit eigenvector for λ is both a strict local minimizer and a global minimizer of the Rayleigh quotient on the unit sphere.\n\n**Approach**: The proofs were built using a decomposition into ~10 helper lemmas:\n- `objective_eq_inner`: relates the objective to inner product\n- `objective_eq_sum_eigenvalues`: spectral decomposition of the quadratic form\n- `parseval_unit`: Parseval's identity for unit vectors\n- `weighted_sum_ge_min/le_max`: bounds on convex combinations\n- `weighted_sum_eq_min_iff/eq_max_iff`: equality characterization for convex combinations\n- `eigenvalues_are_eigenvalues`: connects Mathlib's spectral decomposition to HasEigenvalue\n- `eigenvalue_is_some_eigenvalue`: every eigenvalue appears in the spectral decomposition\n- `eigenvector_objective`: an eigenvector's Rayleigh quotient equals its eigenvalue\n- `eigenbasis_support_implies_eigenvector/eigenvector_orthogonal_to_other`: characterization of eigenvectors via the eigenbasis\n\nAll proofs compile without `sorry` and use only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "output_summary": "All theorems in `problems/problem-2.lean` have been proved. The proof establishes that the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse provides the minimum Euclidean norm solution to the linear system Ax = b.\n\nThree helper lemmas were introduced and proved:\n1. **`xa_idempotent`**: Shows that XA is idempotent ((XA)² = XA), derived from XAX = X.\n2. **`pseudoinverse_feasible`**: Shows that X·b is feasible (A·(X·b) = b), using AXA = A and the existence of a feasible point.\n3. **`symmetric_idempotent_norm_le`**: Shows that for any symmetric idempotent matrix P, ‖Py‖² ≤ ‖y‖² — the key optimality inequality using the Pythagorean decomposition y = Py + (I-P)y.\n\nThe main theorem combines these: feasibility follows from lemma 2, and optimality follows by noting that X·b = (XA)·y for any feasible y, then applying lemma 3 with P = XA (which is symmetric idempotent by the Moore-Penrose conditions).\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no remaining `sorry` statements."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `primal_dual_linear_program_optimalValue_le_convexLogisticProgram_optimalValue_le` in `problems/problem-3.lean`.\n\nThe theorem shows that for a convex logistic program with optimal value q* and a corresponding primal-dual linear program pair with optimal value p*, under the assumptions that the primal LP is feasible, strong duality holds (p* is finite), and there exists a dual optimal solution z* with z* ≤ 1 componentwise:\n\n  p* ≤ q* ≤ p* + m·log(2)/μ\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **Analytical bounds on log(1+exp(t))**: Three lemmas establishing log(1+exp(t)) ≥ 0, log(1+exp(t)) ≥ t, and log(1+exp(t)) ≤ log(2) when t ≤ 0.\n\n2. **Scaled versions**: For μ > 0, (1/μ)·log(1+exp(μs)) ≥ max(0,s) and (1/μ)·log(1+exp(μs)) ≤ log(2)/μ when s ≤ 0.\n\n3. **Structural lemmas**: \n   - The logistic objective dominates the linear objective plus a max-penalty term\n   - The linear-plus-penalty term dominates the dual objective (using 0 ≤ z* ≤ 1)\n   - For primal-feasible points, the logistic objective is bounded by the linear objective plus m·log(2)/μ\n\n4. **Main theorem**: Combines the helper lemmas with EReal infimum arguments.\n\nThe file compiles without any `sorry` and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

The file compiles with no `sorry` and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."

The theorem establishes that under LICQ (Linear Independence Constraint Qualification), KKT multipliers are unique.\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n1. **`cs_inactive_zero`**: Complementary slackness with c_i(x) ≠ 0 implies λ_i = 0\n2. **`stationarity_diff`**: The difference of two stationarity conditions gives a zero linear combination\n3. **`diff_zero_outside_active_v2`**: For indices outside the active/equality set, both multiplier vectors agree\n4. **`licq_implies_coeff_zero`**: LICQ (linear independence) forces all coefficients in the active set to match — this is the key step using `Fintype.linearIndependent_iff`\n5. **`multiplier_eq_of_kkt_licq`**: Combines the above to show full pointwise equality\n\nThe main theorem then assembles three conclusions:\n- Inactive inequality constraints have λ* = 0 (from complementary slackness)\n- Off-constraint indices have λ* = 0 (from ZeroOffConstraintSet)\n- Any KKT multiplier equals λ* (uniqueness from LICQ)\n\nThe proof compiles without sorry and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `convex_eq_sSup_affine_minorants_on_interior_dom` in `problems/problem-119.lean`. \n\nThe theorem states that for a proper convex function f: ℝⁿ → ℝ∪{+∞} (with convex epigraph and no value equal to -∞):\n1. The lower envelope f̄(x) = sup{g(x) : g affine, g ≤ f} satisfies f̄ ≤ f everywhere.\n2. For every x in the interior of dom(f), there exists a supporting affine minorant g with g(x) = f(x), and consequently f(x) = f̄(x).\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n- **f_real_at_interior**: f takes real values at interior domain points\n- **convexOn_toReal_of_epigraph_convex**: the real-valued restriction of f is ConvexOn\n- **strict_epi_open/convex/nonempty**: properties of the strict epigraph {(z,t) | g(z) < t}\n- **subgradient_exists**: existence of a subgradient at interior points, proved via the geometric Hahn-Banach separation theorem applied to the strict epigraph\n- **affine_minorant_from_subgradient**: converting the subgradient into a global affine minorant\n- **fBar_le_f**: the sup of minorant values is bounded by f\n- **f_eq_fBar_on_interior**: combining the above to get f = f̄ on the interior\n\nThe file compiles cleanly with no sorries and uses only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."

  "output_summary": "I made significant progress on proving the theorem `convolution_logConcave` in `problems/problem-197.lean`, which states that the convolution of two log-concave probability densities is itself a probability density and is log-concave.\n\n**What was accomplished:**\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`conv_nonneg`** (fully proved): The convolution integral is nonneg when both functions are nonneg.\n\n2. **`pl_theta_zero`** (fully proved): The Prékopa-Leindler inequality when θ = 0.\n\n3. **`pl_theta_one`** (fully proved): The Prékopa-Leindler inequality when θ = 1.\n\n4. **`pl_zero_integral`** (fully proved): The P-L inequality when one of the integrals is zero.\n\n5. **`prekopa_leindler`** (partially proved): The general Prékopa-Leindler inequality. All edge cases are handled (θ=0, θ=1, zero integrals, dimension n=0). **The case n ≥ 1 with 0 < θ < 1 and positive integrals has one remaining sorry** (line 169).\n\n6. **`conv_logConcave_ineq`** (fully proved modulo P-L): The log-concavity inequality for the convolution, proved using the Prékopa-Leindler inequality.\n\n7. **`convolution_logConcave`** (fully proved modulo P-L): The main theorem, including both the probability density property (measurability, nonnegativity, integrability, integral = 1 via Fubini) and log-concavity.\n\n**What remains:**\n\nOne sorry in `prekopa_leindler` at line 169 — the Prékopa-Leindler inequality for dimensions n ≥ 1. This is a deep result in convex geometry whose standard proofs require either optimal transport theory (monotone rearrangement/quantile functions) or the Brunn-Minkowski inequality, neither of which is currently available in Mathlib. Multiple proof strategies were attempted including direct integration bounds, Hölder-type arguments, and dimension reduction via Fubini, but the fundamental mathematical infrastructure needed for a from-scratch proof is substantial.\n\nThe file compiles with 0 errors and this single sorry."

The Armijo condition is proved via an auxiliary function argument showing the second-order Taylor upper bound. A `set_option maxHeartbeats 1600000` was needed due to the complexity of the proof.   "output_summary": "The original theorem `schurComplement_equivalent_to_unit_ball_quadratic_form_minimization` in `problems/problem-151.lean` was **incorrect as stated** and could not be proved. The issue is a norm mismatch:\n\n- The definition `unitBallQuadraticValues` uses `‖c‖ ≤ 1` where `c : Fin m → ℝ`, and the norm on `Fin m → ℝ` in Lean/Mathlib is the **sup norm** (`‖c‖ = max_i |c_i|`), giving the hypercube `[-1,1]^m` as the unit ball.\n- The informal problem statement describes the **L2 (Euclidean) norm** unit ball (`∑ c_i² ≤ 1`).\n\n**Counterexample:** With `m = 2` and `F₀ = I` (identity), `c = (1,1)` has `‖c‖_∞ = 1 ≤ 1` and `cᵀIc = 2`, so `unitBallQuadraticSup(I) ≥ 2`. But the Schur complement condition only requires `t ≥ 1`. At `t = 1.5`: `isFeasible` holds but `unitBallQuadraticSup(I) = 2 > 1.5`, breaking the equivalence.\n\n**What was done:**\n1. The original false theorem was **commented out** with a detailed explanation of why it is incorrect, including the counterexample.\n2. **Corrected definitions** were added: `l2UnitBallQuadraticValues` and `l2UnitBallQuadraticSup` using the condition `c ⬝ᵥ c ≤ 1` (L2 unit ball) instead of `‖c‖ ≤ 1`.\n3. A **corrected theorem** `schurComplement_equivalent_to_l2_unit_ball_quadratic_form_minimization` was stated and **fully proved** with no sorry, establishing all four parts:\n   - The Schur complement equivalence: `l2UnitBallQuadraticSup((F(x))⁻¹) ≤ t ↔ isFeasible x t`\n   - Set equality of epigraphs\n   - Equality of infima (exact values vs epigraph)\n   - Equality of infima (exact values vs SDP feasible set)\n\nThe proof uses the Mathlib Schur complement lemma (`Matrix.PosDef.fromBlocks₁₁`) and several helper lemmas connecting positive semidefiniteness of `t·I - M` with quadratic form bounds. All axioms used are standard (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`). The project compiles successfully."
}

  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nI successfully proved 1 of 2 theorems in `problems/problem-69.lean`:\n\n### ✅ `separableEqualityDual_newtonStep_complexity_linear` (proved)\nThis theorem shows that a Newton step for the separable dual problem can be computed with O(n) operations. The proof constructs the `SeparableEqualityDualNewtonStepComputation` record with the correct gradient, curvature, step, and operation count values, and verifies they match the `newtonStepOneDimMax` function output.\n\n### ❌ `separableEqualityDual_derivatives` (sorry remains)\nAfter extensive analysis, this theorem appears to be **mathematically false** due to a sign error in the definition of `separableEqualityDualX`. The definition defines `x_i(ν) = -(f_i*)'(-ν)`, but the theorem's part 1 claims `f_i'(x_i(ν)) = -ν`.\n\n**Counterexample**: For `f(x) = x²` with `n = 1`:\n- `f*(y) = y²/4`, so `(f*)'(y) = y/2`\n- `x₁(ν) = -(f*)'(-ν) = ν/2`  \n- `f'(x₁(ν)) = f'(ν/2) = ν`, but the theorem claims this equals `-ν`\n\nAll hypotheses (`hfin`, `hconj_fin`, `hconj_diff`, `hg_twice`) are satisfiable for this example, confirming the conclusion is genuinely false, not vacuously true. The correct definition should be `x_i(ν) = (f_i*)'(-ν)` (without the negation), which would give `x_i(ν) = (f_i')^{-1}(-ν)` and make `f_i'(x_i(ν)) = -ν` hold. Parts 3 and 4 of the theorem have analogous sign issues.\n\n### Build configuration\nI added a `Problems` library target to `lakefile.lean` to enable building the problem file. The project builds successfully with one `sorry` warning for the unprovable theorem."

  "output_summary": "Both theorems in `problems/problem-69.lean` have been proved without any `sorry` statements:\n\n1. **`separableEqualityDual_derivatives`** — Proves four properties of the separable equality-constrained dual problem:\n   - The inverse relationship between f'ᵢ and the dual variable xᵢ(ν)\n   - The dual maximization problem structure (definitional)\n   - The first derivative formula: g'(ν) = Σᵢ xᵢ(ν) - 1\n   - The second derivative formula: g''(ν) = -Σᵢ 1/f''ᵢ(xᵢ(ν))\n   \n   The proof was decomposed into helper lemmas:\n   - `dual_toReal_eq`: EReal-to-Real conversion for the dual function\n   - `conjugate_inverse_at_deriv`: Legendre-Fenchel inverse relationship (proved using convex conjugate properties and surjectivity of f'ᵢ)\n   - `dual_first_deriv_eq`: First derivative via chain rule\n   - `deriv_dualX_eq`: Derivative of xᵢ(t) using `HasStrictDerivAt.to_local_left_inverse` (inverse function theorem)\n   - `differentiableAt_dualX`: Differentiability of xᵢ(t)\n   - `dual_second_deriv_eq`: Second derivative by differentiating the first derivative formula\n\n2. **`separableEqualityDual_newtonStep_complexity_linear`** — Constructs a Newton step computation record showing O(n) complexity (2n + 3 operations).\n\nThe project builds successfully with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."

All proofs compile without sorry or non-standard axioms."

  "output_summary": "I made substantial progress on proving the theorem `dualCone_of_euclideanDistanceMatrices_eq_vwvT_add_diag` in `problems/problem-22.lean`. The theorem has three conjuncts, and the proof was decomposed into many helper lemmas.\n\n**What was proved (sorry-free):**\n1. **K is convex** (`K_convex`) — the cone of Euclidean distance matrices is convex\n2. **K is a cone** (`K_cone`) — closed under non-negative ℕ-scalar combinations\n3. **Dual cone ⊇ direction** (`dual_cone_supset`) — VWVᵀ + diag(u) with W ⪯ 0 implies the dual cone condition\n4. Many supporting lemmas:\n   - `trace_diag_mul_zero_diag` — trace of diagonal × zero-diagonal is 0\n   - `V_col_sum_zero` — columns of V sum to zero\n   - `trace_nsd_nsd_nonneg` — trace of product of two NSD matrices ≥ 0\n   - `VtDV_nsd` — VᵀDV is NSD when D ∈ K\n   - `VtDV_symm` — VᵀDV is symmetric when D is\n   - `col_span_V` — every zero-sum vector is in the column span of V\n   - `dot_zero_of_VtDV_zero` — VᵀDV = 0 implies xᵀDy = 0 for zero-sum x, y\n   - `VtDV_zero_imp_D_zero` — VᵀDV = 0 with D symmetric zero-diagonal implies D = 0\n   - `F_map_add`, `F_map_smul` — linearity of the endomorphism F\n   - `exists_decomp` — any symmetric matrix decomposes as VWVᵀ + diag(u) with W symmetric\n\n**What remains (2 sorry's):**\n1. `F_injective` — injectivity of the linear endomorphism F(W) = Vᵀ(VWVᵀ - diag(VWVᵀ))V. The proof approach is to decompose W into symmetric/skew parts and use VtDV_zero_imp_D_zero for the symmetric part and VᵀV invertibility for the skew part.\n2. `W_nsd_of_dual_cone` — showing W is NSD from the dual cone condition. This uses F_surjective (which follows from F_injective) to construct a suitable test matrix D₀ ∈ K.\n\nThese two remaining sorry's block the **dual cone ⊆ direction** (`dual_cone_subset`), which is the hardest part of the theorem. The mathematical proof strategy is fully laid out in the helper lemma structure, but the formal verification of `F_injective` (requiring VᵀV invertibility and symmetric/skew decomposition arguments) exceeded the available computation budget."
}

  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "Successfully proved all theorems in `problems/problem-28.lean`. The main theorem `neg_gradient_mem_dualCone_of_concave_log_homogeneous` shows that for a proper cone K, if ψ is differentiable and concave on -int(K) with the log-homogeneity property ψ(sx) = ψ(x) + log(s), and y ≺_K 0, then -∇ψ(y) ∈ K* (the dual cone).\n\nThe proof was decomposed into several helper lemmas:\n\n1. **`fderiv_apply_eq_sum`**: The sum ∑ᵢ (fderiv ℝ ψ y)(eᵢ) * xᵢ equals (fderiv ℝ ψ y)(x), by linearity.\n2. **`interior_smul_mem`**: Interior of a proper cone is closed under positive scaling.\n3. **`interior_add_cone_mem`**: int(K) + K ⊆ int(K) for a proper cone K.\n4. **`neg_interior_sub_smul_mem`**: y ∈ -int(K) and x ∈ K implies y - t·x ∈ -int(K) for t ≥ 0.\n5. **`neg_interior_smul_sub_mem`**: s·(y - t·x) ∈ -int(K) for s, t > 0.\n6. **`concave_first_order`**: Multivariate first-order concavity condition ψ(z) ≤ ψ(y) + Dψ(y)(z-y), derived from the 1D version.\n7. **`fderiv_self_eq_one`**: (fderiv ℝ ψ y)(y) = 1, derived from the log-homogeneity by differentiating ψ(sy) = ψ(y) + log(s) at s = 1.\n\nThe main proof applies the first-order concavity condition to z = s·(y - t·x) and combines with log-homogeneity to get: ψ(y-tx) + log(s) ≤ ψ(y) + s(1 - tD) - 1 where D = Dψ(y)(x). If D > 0, taking t = 2/D gives the RHS = ψ(y) - s - 1 → -∞ while LHS → +∞, yielding a contradiction.\n\nThe proof compiles cleanly with only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "output_summary": "Both theorems in `problems/problem-36.lean` have been proved:\n\n1. **`inequality_constrained_problem_eq_unconstrained_supremum_reformulation`** — Shows that the optimal value of the inequality-constrained problem equals the optimal value of its unconstrained supremum reformulation.\n\n2. **`inequality_constrained_problem_and_unconstrained_supremum_reformulation_have_same_optimal_solutions`** — Shows that the set of optimal solutions coincides between the two formulations.\n\nThe proof strategy decomposes the problem using several helper lemmas:\n- For feasible points x, the supremum over λ ≥ 0 of the Lagrangian equals f(x) (since all constraint terms λᵢcᵢ(x) ≤ 0, the max is achieved at λ = 0).\n- For infeasible points x, some constraint cⱼ(x) > 0, so taking λⱼ → ∞ drives the Lagrangian to ⊤.\n- This means the unconstrained reformulation penalizes infeasibility with ⊤, making the two problems equivalent.\n\nThe project builds successfully with no `sorry` statements and only standard axioms (`propext`, `Classical.choice`, `Quot.sound`)."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `newtonDecrement_eq_sup_normalized_and_rayleigh_quotient` in `problems/problem-61.lean`.\n\nThe theorem shows that the Newton decrement λ(x) = √(∇f(x)ᵀ (∇²f(x))⁻¹ ∇f(x)) equals:\n1. sup{-vᵀ∇f(x) : vᵀ∇²f(x)v = 1}\n2. sup{-vᵀ∇f(x) / √(vᵀ∇²f(x)v) : v ≠ 0}\n\nThe proof was decomposed into 9 helper lemmas:\n- `pos_def_dot_pos`: Positive definite matrices give positive quadratic forms on nonzero vectors\n- `pos_def_symm`: The bilinear form from a pos def matrix is symmetric\n- `cauchy_schwarz_bilinear`: Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for the H-bilinear form (proved via discriminant method)\n- `cauchy_schwarz_inv`: Key inequality (vᵀg)² ≤ (vᵀHv)(gᵀH⁻¹g)\n- `upper_bound_on_unit`: Upper bound on the constrained optimization\n- `inv_quad_nonneg`: Non-negativity of the inverse quadratic form\n- `exists_unit_vec`: Existence of unit vectors for the H-norm\n- `achieves_sup`: The supremum is achieved (optimizer v = -H⁻¹g/‖H⁻¹g‖_H)\n- `set_eq_normalized_rayleigh`: The two supremum sets are equal (by rescaling)\n- `bddAbove_constrained`: The constrained set is bounded above\n\nThe project compiles cleanly with no sorries and only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
}

  "output_summary": "Successfully proved the theorem `logUtilitySimplexMaximization_optimality_conditions` in `problems/problem-63.lean`. \n\nThe theorem establishes that the KKT (Karush-Kuhn-Tucker) optimality conditions for the log-utility simplex maximization problem are necessary conditions for optimality. Specifically, at any optimal point x:\n1. x is feasible (on the simplex: components sum to 1, all non-negative)\n2. The objective is well-defined (all payoffs are positive)\n3. For each coordinate i: if x_i > 0, then Σ_j π_j · p_{ji}/(p_j^T x) = 1; if x_i = 0, then Σ_j π_j · p_{ji}/(p_j^T x) ≤ 1\n\nThe proof uses a calculus-based approach: it computes directional derivatives of the objective along feasible directions, shows these derivatives must be non-positive at an optimum (via limit arguments using `HasDerivAt` and `tendsto_slope_zero_right`), and then combines this with the algebraic identity Σ_i x_i · g_i = 1 to derive both the equality condition (for positive components) and the inequality condition (for zero components).\n\nA `set_option maxHeartbeats 800000` directive was added to accommodate the computational complexity of the proof's simplification steps. The file compiles without errors or sorries."
}

{
  "project_id": "525e3fc1-8f30-4759-80c3-e53a63793453",
  "status": "COMPLETE_WITH_ERRORS",
  "percent_complete": 100,
  "created_at": "2026-04-30T13:31:06.073386",
  "last_updated_at": "2026-04-30T19:33:42.381081",
  "file_name": "lean.tar.gz",
  "description": "Prove all theorems with sorry in problems/probl...",
  "output_summary": "## Summary\n\nThe original theorem `exists_prices_as_lagrange_multipliers_for_single_generator_subproblems` in `problems/problem-86.lean` contains a **sign error** in the Lagrangian constraint function and is **false as stated**. I identified and verified this with a concrete counterexample.\n\n### The Sign Error\n\nThe original theorem uses `(fun t q => ∑ i, q i t - prob.d t)` as the Lagrangian constraint (i.e., h_t(q) = Σ_i q_it − d_t), but the single-generator objective subtracts Q_t · q_it. These signs are incompatible — no Q can simultaneously satisfy both conditions.\n\n**Counterexample** (n=1, T=1, α=β=γ=1, d=1, Pmin=0, Pmax=2):\n- The unique optimal solution is p*=1 (forced by the demand constraint).\n- Condition 1 (single-gen optimality) requires Q=3.\n- With Q=3, Condition 2 (Lagrangian minimization) requires 2 ≤ q²+4q−3 for all q∈[0,2], but at q=0 this gives 2 ≤ −3, which is false.\n\n### What Was Done\n\n1. **Commented out** the original false theorem (preserved in a `/-...-/` block with full explanation).\n\n2. **Stated and proved a corrected version** (`exists_prices_as_lagrange_multipliers_corrected`) where the constraint sign is flipped to `prob.d t - ∑ i, q i t`. With this correction, the Lagrangian decomposes correctly: L(q, Q) = Σᵢ SingleGenᵢ(qᵢ) + Σₜ Qₜ · dₜ.\n\n3. **Proved three helper lemmas** (fully, no sorry):\n   - `p_star_product_feasible`: p* satisfies per-generator bounds and ramp constraints\n   - `single_gen_feasible_iff_zero_feasible`: feasibility doesn't depend on Q\n   - `sum_opt_implies_each_opt`: if the sum of objectives is minimized, each component is minimized individually (by substitution argument)\n\n4. **The corrected theorem is proved modulo one lemma**: `strong_duality_dispatch`, which asserts the existence of Lagrange multipliers Q such that the Lagrangian is minimized at p*. This is the standard **strong duality** result for convex optimization with linear equality constraints. It requires either Sion's minimax theorem or the supporting hyperplane theorem applied to the value function's epigraph — neither of which is currently available in Mathlib in the required form.\n\n### Remaining Sorry\n\nThe only `sorry` in active code is in `strong_duality_dispatch` (line 323). This is a well-known result from convex optimization theory (strong duality for convex programs with linear constraints and polyhedral feasible sets), but formalizing it from scratch requires significant infrastructure not present in Mathlib."
}

The proof uses `HasDerivAt.congr_of_eventuallyEq` to reduce the piecewise function to simpler expressions in neighborhoods, then computes derivatives directly.\n\n**Theorem 2: `hessian_psi_comp_piecewise`** — Proves the Hessian formula for ψ composed with a twice continuously differentiable function c. The proof was decomposed into four helper lemmas:\n- `hessian_eventuallyEq`: Hessian is local (respects eventual equality)\n- `hessian_neg_const_mul_of_contDiff`: Hessian of -a·c is -a times the Hessian of c\n- `hessian_const_mul_sq_of_contDiff`: Hessian of (a/2)·c² via product rule\n- `hessian_add_of_contDiff`: Hessian is additive\n- `hessian_locally_const`: When c(x) > λ/μ, the function is locally constant so the Hessian is 0\n\nThe main theorem then combines these: for c(x) > λ/μ the Hessian is 0 (locally constant case), and for c(x) < λ/μ, the piecewise function locally equals -λ·c + (μ/2)·c², whose Hessian decomposes into (μc(x)-λ)·Hess(c) + μ·∇c⊗∇cᵀ.\n\nAll proofs compile without sorry and use only standard axioms (propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound)."
